## I / I

===Original===
William Tully was a little over three-and-twenty when he emerged from the chrysalis stage of his clerkdom and became a Social Reformer. His life and doings until the age of twenty-three, had given small promise of the distinction of his future career; from a mild-mannered, pale-faced and undersized boy he had developed into a mild-mannered, pale-faced little adult standing five-foot-five in his boots. Educated at a small private school in the suburbs of London, his record for conduct was practically spotless and he once took a prize for Divinity; further, to the surprise and relief of his preceptors, he managed to scrape through the Senior Cambridge Local Examination before he was transferred to a desk in the office of a London insurance company. His preceptor-in-chief, in a neatly-written certificate, assured his future employers that they would find him painstaking and obedient﻿—and William, for the first six years of his engagement, lived up to the character given him. His mother, a sharp-eyed, masterful woman, had brought him up to be painstaking and obedient; it might be said with truth that as long as she lived he did not know how to be otherwise. It is true he disliked his office superiors vaguely, for the restrictions they placed upon his wishes﻿—just as, for the same reason, he vaguely disliked his mother; but his wishes being indeterminate and his ambition nonexistent, his vague dislike never stiffened into active resentment.

It would seem that the supreme effort of passing his Cambridge Local had left him mentally exhausted for a season; at any rate, from the conclusion of his schooldays till he made the acquaintance of Faraday, his reading was practically confined to romantic and humorous literature. He was a regular patron of the fiction department of the municipal lending library and did not disdain to spend modestly on periodicals of the type of *Snappy Bits*. He was unable to spend more than modestly because his earnings, with the exception of a small sum for fares and pocket-money, were annexed by his mother each Saturday as a matter of normal routine. The manner of her annexation made discussion singularly difficult; and if William ever felt stirrings of rebellion over the weekly cash delivery he was careful never to betray them.

With his colleagues of the office Tully was a negligible quantity. He was not unpopular﻿—it was merely that he did not matter. His mother’s control of the family funds was no doubt in part accountable for his comrades’ neglect of his society; but his own habits and manners were still more largely to blame, since besides being painstaking and obedient he was unobtrusive and diffident. There was once a project on foot in the office to take him out and make him drunk﻿—but nothing came of it because no one was sufficiently interested in William to give up an evening to the job.

===Chinese===
威廉·塔利（William Tully）从职员生涯的茧蛹里钻出来、摇身变为社会改革家的时候，年方二十三岁出头。他在二十三岁以前的经历与行止，几乎看不出日后事业有什么辉煌的气象：从一个性情温和、面色苍白、身材矮小的男孩，长成了一个性情温和、面色苍白的小个子成人，穿上靴子身高五英尺五英寸。他在伦敦郊区一所小小的私立学校受教育，操行记录几乎无懈可击，还拿过一次神学奖；此外，令诸位恩师又惊又慰的是，他在被调往伦敦一家保险公司的办公桌之前，勉强蹭过了剑桥地方考试的高级等第。他的首席恩师在一封字迹工整的鉴定书里，向未来的雇主保证他们将会发现此人勤勉而驯顺——而在受雇的头六年里，威廉也果然不负这句考语。他的母亲是个目光尖利、专断强悍的女人，从小把他调教得勤勉而驯顺；可以毫不夸张地说，只要她还活着一天，他就不知道除此之外还能怎样做人。诚然，他也朦朦胧胧地不喜欢办公室里的上司，因为他们对他的愿望多方限制——出于同样的缘故，他也朦朦胧胧地不喜欢他的母亲；但他的愿望本就含混不清，抱负更是子虚乌有，于是那点朦胧的不满始终不曾凝固成积极的怨恨。

看来，通过剑桥地方考试那一番登峰造极的努力，曾叫他的头脑疲惫了一阵子；无论如何，从学生时代结束到结识法拉第（Faraday）为止，他的阅读实际上不出言情与诙谐文学的范围。他是市立流通图书馆小说部的常客，也不吝于省吃俭用地花点小钱，买《俏皮小品》（Snappy Bits）一类的刊物。他之所以只能省吃俭用，是因为每逢星期六，他的薪水——除了留出车马费和零用的小小一笔——都要照例被母亲一并没收。她那没收的方式令商量二字全然无从谈起；倘若威廉对每星期上缴现款一事也曾心头涌起过反抗的暗流，他也小心翼翼，从不让它们露出分毫。

在办公室的同事中间，塔利是个可以忽略不计的存在。他并非不得人心——只是无足轻重罢了。同事们懒得同他来往，一半固然要归咎于他母亲对家庭财源的大权独揽，但更大的责任还在他自己的习性与做派：因为除了勤勉驯顺之外，他还无声无息、腼腆怕羞。办公室里一度有过一个计划，要把他带出去灌醉——终归不了了之，因为对威廉的兴趣，还没有大到谁肯为他搭上一个晚上。

===Original===
The crisis in his hitherto well-ordered life came when his mother died suddenly. This was in October 1910. William had gone to the office as usual that morning, leaving his mother apparently in her usual health; he returned in the evening to blinds already drawn down. A neighbour (female) was in waiting in the sitting-room and broke the great news with a sense of its importance and her own; she took William’s hand, told him with sniffs that it was the will of the Lord, and entered into clinical details. William sat down rather suddenly when he realized that there would be no one in future to annex his weekly earnings; then, shocked by his lack of filial feeling, he endeavoured to produce an emotion more suited to the solemn occasion. Disconcerted by a want of success which he feared was apparent to his audience, he fidgeted, dry-eyed and awkward﻿—and finally, all things considered, acted well and wisely by demanding to be left alone. To his relief the demand was accepted as reasonable and proper in the first moments of his grief; the sympathizer withdrew, wiping her eyes﻿—unnecessarily﻿—and hoping that God would support him. He locked the door stealthily and stared at his mother’s armchair; he was a little afraid of its emptiness, he was also shocked and excited. He knew instinctively that more was to happen, that life from now on would be something new and different.﻿ ﻿… The armchair was empty; the masterful little woman who had borne him, slapped him, managed him and cowed him﻿—the masterful little woman was dead! There was no one now to whom he was accountable; no one of whom he was afraid.﻿ ﻿… He walked on tiptoe round the tiny room, feeling strangely and pleasantly alive.

The next day increased the sense of his newfound importance; his mother had died rich, as he and she understood riches. She had trusted her son in nothing, not even with the knowledge of her income, and after the stinting and scraping to which she had accustomed him he was amazed to find himself master of a hundred and fifty pounds a year, the interest on capital gradually and carefully invested. In his amazement﻿—at first incredulous﻿—he trod on air, while his mind wandered hazily over the glorious possibilities of opulent years to come; the only alloy in his otherwise supreme content being the necessity for preserving (at least until the funeral was over) a decent appearance of dejection. He felt, too, the need of a friend in whom to confide, someone of his own age and standing before whom it would not be needful to keep up the appearance of dejection and who would not be shocked at the babblings of his stirred and exultant soul; and it was this natural longing for a confidant which, on the day following his mother’s funeral, led to the beginning of his friendship with his fellow-clerk, Faraday.

The head of his department, meeting him in the passage, had said a few perfunctory and conventional words of condolence﻿—whereto William had muttered a sheepish “Thank you, sir,” and escaped as soon as might be. The familiar office after his four days’ estrangement from it affected him curiously and unpleasantly; he felt his newly-acquired sense of importance slipping gradually away from him, felt himself becoming once again the underling and creature of routine﻿—the William Tully, obedient and painstaking, who had earned from his childhood the favourable contempt of his superiors. It was borne in on him as the hours went by that it was not enough to accept good fortune﻿—good fortune had to be made use of; and he began to make plans in an irregular, tentative fashion, biting the end of his pen and neglecting his work. Should he chuck the office? and if he chucked it, what then?﻿ ﻿… Here imagination failed him; his life had been so ordered, so bound down and directed by others, that even his desires were tamed to the wishes of others and left to himself he could not tell what he desired. The need for sympathy and guidance became imperative; driving him, when the other occupants of the room had departed for lunch, to unbosom himself to Faraday.

===Chinese===
他那至今秩序井然的生活，危机出现在母亲猝然去世之时。那是 1910 年 10 月。那天早晨威廉照常去办公室上班，离家时母亲看样子一如平日康健；傍晚回家，百叶窗已经拉下了。起居室里候着一位邻居（女性），怀着对这消息之重大——以及她本人之重大——的双重自觉，宣布了这条大新闻；她握住威廉的手，抽着鼻子告诉他这是主的旨意，随即进入临床细节的叙述。待意识到今后再没有人来没收他的周薪时，威廉相当突然地坐了下去；随即他又为自己的缺乏孝心而骇然，竭力挤出一种更配得上这庄严场合的情绪来。那情绪迟迟挤不出来，他生怕这败绩已被旁观者看破，于是干着眼睛，局促不安地扭动——末了，通盘权衡之下，他要求独自待着，这一手倒是做得又得体又明智。令他宽慰的是，这要求在丧亲之初被视为合情合理、理所当然；那位吊唁者退了出去，抹着眼睛——大可不必——并祈愿上帝扶持他。他悄悄锁上门，怔怔望着母亲的扶手椅；那椅子空荡荡的，他有点怕，又觉得震惊，又觉得兴奋。他本能地知道，还有更多的事将要发生，从今往后的生活将是崭新而不同的。……扶手椅空了；那个生了他、打过他、管着他、镇着他的专断的小妇人——那个专断的小妇人死了！如今再没有他要交代的人，再没有他要害怕的人了。……他踮着脚尖在这小小的房间里转来转去，只觉得自己前所未有地活着，说不出的惬意。

第二天，他这份新发觉的身价感愈发重了；母亲死得富有——照他和她对富有的理解。她对儿子事事不信，连自己的进项有多少都不让他知道；过惯了她使他习以为常的省吃俭用的日子之后，他惊愕地发现自己竟成了一年一百五十英镑的主人——那是历年小心谨慎地积攒投资下来的一笔本钱的利息。在惊愕之中——起初简直不敢相信——他飘飘然如在云端，心里朦朦胧胧地盘算着未来富足岁月的种种美景；这份登峰造极的满足里唯一的杂质，是不得不维持一副像样的悲戚面孔（至少到丧礼办完为止）。他还感到需要一个可以倾吐的朋友，一个同龄同辈的人，在他面前不必再端着悲戚的架势，也不会被他那翻腾雀跃的灵魂的絮语吓着；正是这寻求知己的天性渴望，在母亲下葬后的第二天，促成了他与其同事法拉第的友谊的开端。

他的科长在走廊里遇见他，敷衍地说了几句客套的吊慰——威廉嗫嚅着道了声「谢谢您，先生」，便尽早溜之大吉。阔别四天之后重回熟悉的办公室，他心里生出一种古怪而不快的感觉；他觉得自己新到手的身价感正一点点溜走，觉得自己又变回了那个听命于人、例行公事的小下属——那个从小就赢得上司们嘉许式轻蔑的威廉·塔利：驯顺而勤勉。随着时间一个钟头一个钟头过去，他渐渐悟到：光接受好运是不够的——好运还得加以利用；于是他开始东一笔西一笔地起草计划，咬着笔头，把正事撇在一边。要不要把办公室这份差事甩了？甩了之后又怎样？……一到这里，想象力便告罄了；他这一生被安排得如此妥帖，被旁人束缚指使得如此周全，连他的欲望都被驯化成了别人的愿望——真让他自己做主，他倒说不出自己到底想要什么。对同情与指引的渴求变得刻不容缓；等到屋里旁的人都去吃午饭了，这渴求便驱使他向法拉第倾吐衷肠。

===Original===
In his longing to talk he would have addressed himself almost to anyone; but on the whole, and in spite of an entire ignorance of his habits and character, he was glad it was Faraday who was left behind to hear him﻿—a newcomer, recently transferred from another branch and, as William realized (if only half-consciously) like himself regarded by their fellow-clerks as a bit of an outsider. A sallow-faced young man, dark-haired and with large hazel eyes, he was neatly garbed as became an insurance clerk; but there was a suggestion of discomfort about his conventional neatness, just as there was a suggestion of effort about his personal cleanliness. He worked hard and steadily; taking no part in the interludes of blithesome chat wherewith his companions enlivened their hours of toil and appearing to be satisfied rather than annoyed by the knowledge of his own isolation. He had spoken to William but two or three times and always in the way of business﻿—nor was his profile bent over a ledger particularly suggestive of sympathy; William’s emotions, however, had reached exploding-point, and the door had hardly closed behind the last of their fellows when he blurted out, “I say,” and Faraday raised his head.

“I say,” William blurted again, “did you know﻿—my mother’s dead?”

“Ah﻿—yes,” said Faraday uncomfortably; he believed he was being appealed to for sympathy, and fidgeted, clearing his throat; “I﻿—I had heard it mentioned. I needn’t say I’m very sorry﻿—extremely.﻿ ﻿… I suppose you were very much attached to her?”

===Chinese===
以他那急于倾诉的渴望，几乎随便逮着谁都行；但总体说来，尽管对法拉第的习性与为人一无所知，威廉还是庆幸留下听他说话的偏偏是此人——一个新来乍到者，不久前才从另一家分行调来，而且——威廉意识到这一点（哪怕只是半自觉）——同他自己一样，被同事们看作多少是个局外人。他是个面色微黄的年轻人，黑发，一双淡褐色的大眼睛；衣着整洁得体，恰合保险公司职员的身份；只是那份中规中矩的整洁里透着几分不自在，正如他身上的干净也透着几分刻意。他工作勤勉而稳定，从不加入同事们用来给劳碌时光添趣的轻快闲谈；对于自己被孤立一事，他知情之余与其说恼火，不如说满意。他同威廉统共说过两三回话，且全为公事——他俯身在账簿上的侧影也丝毫引不起人的同情心；然而威廉的情绪已然涨到了爆点，最后一个同事的身影刚在门口消失，他就脱口喊了一声：「我说——」法拉第抬起了头。

「我说，」威廉又冒出一句，「你知道么——我母亲去世了？」

「啊——是的。」法拉第说着，颇不自在；他以为人家是在向他求取同情，便清着嗓子，坐立不安；「我——我听人提起过。我非常遗憾——极其遗憾，这不必多说。……想必你同她感情很深吧？」

===Original===
William reflected for a moment and then answered honestly, “No.”

“Indeed!” Faraday returned, surprised as well as uncomfortable. Not knowing what further to say, his eyes went back to the ledger and the conversation languished. It was William who resumed it﻿—wondering at the difficulty of expressing his bubbling emotions.

“I don’t mean to say,” he explained with a twinge of remorse, “that I had anything to complain of. My mother always did her duty by me. But we weren’t what you might call sympathetic.”

“Indeed!” Faraday repeated﻿—still at sea as to the motive of the conversation.

===Chinese===
威廉想了想，然后老老实实地回答：「不深。」

「是这样！」法拉第应道，又惊讶又不自在。他想不出下面还该说什么，眼睛便回到账簿上，谈话就此蔫了下去。重新拾起话头的还是威廉——他一面纳闷，自己这翻腾沸腾的情绪怎么这般难以出口。

「我不是想说，」他带着一丝悔意解释道，「我有什么可抱怨的。我母亲对我向来尽了她的本分。只是我们娘儿俩，算不上您所谓的情投意合。」

「是这样！」法拉第重复了一句——对这场谈话的用意仍旧一头雾水。

===Original===
“It was unfortunate,” William went on, “but it couldn’t be helped. I am sure she was a very good woman.” (He said this with the more confidence because, from his childhood up, he had always associated goodness with lack of amiability.) “But that wasn’t what I wanted to say. What I wanted to say was, she has left me a good deal of money.”

“Indeed?” said Faraday for the third time; adding something about “congratulation.” He hoped the episode was over﻿—but William was only beginning.

“I’ve been wondering,” he said, “what I should do﻿—now that I’m independent. I don’t want to go on like this. It’s a waste﻿—when you’ve got money. But I don’t know how to set about things.﻿ ﻿… If someone would put me in the way!”

Faraday, raising his eyes from the ledger, met the wistful appeal in William’s and imagined himself enlightened.

===Chinese===
「这很不幸，」威廉接着说，「可也没法子。我敢肯定她是个非常好的女人。」（他说这话格外有底气，因为从小到大，他一向把「好」与「不和气」联在一起。）「不过这不是我要说的。我要说的是，她留给了我好一笔钱。」

「是吗？」法拉第第三次说道，又添了句「恭喜」之类的客套。他指望这桩事到此为止——可威廉才刚开头呢。

「我一直在琢磨，」他说，「如今我自立了，该干点什么。我不想再这样过下去。这是浪费——手里有了钱还这样。可我不知道该从何下手。……要是有人肯给我指条道儿就好了！」

法拉第从账簿上抬起眼，正撞上威廉眼里恳切的求告，自以为恍然大悟了。

===Original===
“I see,” he said interrogatively; “then you haven’t got your living to earn﻿—you are not tied here any longer? You can direct your own life and take up any line you choose?”

“Yes,” William assented, pleased with the phrase; “I can direct my own life﻿—certainly.”

“Which,” Faraday suggested, “was difficult for you before?”

“Very,” said William emphatically.

“And,” the other went on, “now that you are your own man you wish to take the line that attracts you and be of some use?”

“Oh, certainly,” William assented again﻿—perhaps a shade less emphatically. So far his ideas had run more upon pleasure than usefulness.

===Chinese===
「我明白了，」他用探询的口气说，「这么说，你不必为生计奔忙——不再受这里的束缚了？你可以自己支配自己的生活，想走哪条路就走哪条路？」

「是啊，」威廉表示同意，对这说法颇为受用；「我能自己支配自己的生活——当然。」

「而这，」法拉第提示道，「从前对你是件难事？」

「难极了。」威廉说得斩钉截铁。

「还有，」对方继续道，「如今你是自己的主人了，你想走一条吸引你的路，做点有用的事？」

「哦，当然。」威廉又表示同意——口气或许略逊了一分坚决。迄今为止，他念头里盘算的享乐多，用处少。

===Original===
Faraday reflected with his chin resting on his hand.

“Why have you asked me?” he demanded suddenly﻿—with the accent strongly on the “me.”

“I know so few people,” William explained humbly. “I mean, of course, people who could give me any ideas.﻿ ﻿… I thought you wouldn’t mind﻿—at least I hoped you wouldn’t.﻿ ﻿… I know it’s unusual﻿—but if you could help me in any way?﻿ ﻿… With suggestions, you know.”

Again Faraday reflected with his chin resting on his hand.

“I could put you,” he said at last, “in touch with people who might help you. I should be very pleased to do so.﻿ ﻿… Of course, I should like to know more of you first﻿—what your views are﻿—”

“Of course,” William agreed vaguely, puzzled partly by the words and partly by the enigmatic manner.

===Chinese===
法拉第手支着下巴，沉吟起来。

「你为什么偏偏来问我？」他突然发问——重音狠狠落在那个「我」字上。

「我认识的人太少了，」威廉谦卑地解释，「当然，我是说能给我出点子的人。……我想你不会介意——至少我希望你不介意。……我知道这不合常例——可你能不能无论如何帮帮我？……出出主意，你知道的。」

法拉第又手支着下巴沉吟起来。

「我可以让你，」他终于开口，「同一些或许帮得上你的人搭上关系。我很乐意这么做。……当然，我想先对你多些了解——你的观点是什么——」

「当然。」威廉含含糊糊地表示同意，一半被这话弄糊涂了，一半被那副神秘莫测的神气弄糊涂了。

===Original===
“If you’ve nothing else to do,” Faraday continued, “perhaps you’ll come round to my rooms tonight for a talk? Say at half-past eight. We could discuss things more comfortably there.”

William, still puzzled by the hint of mystery in his manner, murmured that he also should be very pleased, and Faraday gave him the address﻿—returning forthwith to his ledger in sign that he considered the incident closed for the present. He had a distinctly authoritative way with him, and William, who would gladly have continued the subject, had perforce to be content with wondering what the night’s discussion and exchange of “views” would bring forth; an evening spent away from home was so rare an event in his life that the prospect of his visit to Faraday’s rooms afforded him food for an afternoon’s busy speculation. His own domicile being in the region of Camberwell, he did not return to it after office hours but whiled away the time by dinner at an Oxford Street Lyons﻿—secretly glorying in the length of his bill and contrasting his power of spending what he liked with the old days of doled-out allowance. He rang down a sovereign at the pay-desk, gathered up his change and strolled out of the building with an air﻿—and at half-past eight precisely found himself outside Faraday’s lodgings in a mournful side-street in Bloomsbury. A shabby maidservant ushered him upstairs to a shabby, paper-strewn room where Faraday, pipe in mouth, rose to greet him.

They were not long in finding out that the invitation had been given and accepted under a misapprehension on both sides. Faraday, as soon as he had settled his guest in a chair, came straight to the point with “Now tell me﻿—how long have you been interested in social questions?”

“In social questions?” William repeated blankly. “I’m afraid I don’t﻿—What sort of questions do you mean?”

===Chinese===
「要是你今晚没别的安排，」法拉第接着说，「不妨到我住处来谈谈？八点半如何。在那儿谈事情可以更自在些。」

威廉对那人神气里透出的神秘意味仍感困惑，喃喃地说自己也非常乐意；法拉第把地址给了他——随即回到账簿上，以此表示他认为此事暂且告一段落。他身上自有一种说一不二的威严气派；威廉本巴不得就这个话题再聊下去，如今也只好满足于悬想今晚这场讨论和「观点」的交流将会结出什么果实。在他一生中，在外面消磨一个晚上是何等稀罕的事，以致对造访法拉第住所的期待，就够他整整一个下午忙忙碌碌地胡思乱想。他自己的住处在坎伯韦尔（Camberwell）一带，下班后他没回去，而是在牛津街（Oxford Street）上一家利昂斯（Lyons）餐馆吃了顿晚饭消磨时光——暗暗为账单之长而得意，把如今想花就花的豪气，同从前按日发放零用钱的日子作了个对比。他在付款台上叮的一声拍下一枚金镑，收起找头，派头十足地踱出店堂——八点半整，他已站在布卢姆斯伯里（Bloomsbury）一条灰扑扑的侧街上、法拉第寓所的门前。一个衣衫寒酸的女仆引他上楼，进了一间同样寒酸、满地废纸的房间；法拉第嘴里叼着烟斗，起身相迎。

两人没用多久便发现：这一邀一允，原是双方各怀的一场误会。法拉第刚把客人安置到椅子上，便开门见山：「现在告诉我——你对社会问题感兴趣有多久了？」

「社会问题？」威廉茫然地重复了一遍。「恐怕我不——你指的是哪一类问题？」

===Original===
It was Faraday’s turn to be taken aback, and, though he did not say it, his eyes looked. “Then what the devil﻿—?” William’s fell before them nervously, and he shifted in his chair like a child detected in a blunder.

“I’m afraid I don’t—” he said again﻿—and halted.

“Then you didn’t know,” his companion queried, “that I am ‘Vindex’ of *The Torch*?”

“I’m afraid not,” muttered William, who had heard neither of one nor the other.

===Chinese===
这回轮到法拉第愕然了；他嘴上虽没说，眼睛却道出了那句话：「那你究竟——？」威廉被那目光看得心虚，眼神败退下来，在椅子里挪动着，像个闯了祸被当场拿住的孩子。

「恐怕我不——」他又说了一遍——便打住了。

「那么你不知道，」对方问道，「我就是《火炬》（The Torch）的『温迪克斯』（Vindex）？」

「恐怕不知道。」威廉喃喃道——这两样他一样也没听说过。

===Original===
“Vindex” of *The Torch* sighed inwardly. He was young, ambitious, fiercely in earnest and ever on the lookout for his Chance; and, the wish being father to the thought, he had momentarily mistaken William for an embodiment of his Chance and dreamed dreams since the morning﻿—dreams of a comrade like-minded and willing to be led, whose newly-inherited riches might be used to endow a periodical that should preach a purer and more violent rebellion even than *The Torch* itself. With the aid of William’s three pounds a week﻿—magnified many times over in the eyes of his eager mind﻿—he had seen himself casting the hated insurance behind him and devoting himself heart and pen to the regeneration of the State and Race by means of the Class War. And lo!﻿—as a couple more searching questions revealed to him﻿—in place of a patron and comrade was a nervous little nincompoop, bewildered at finding himself for the first time out of leading-strings, to whom a hundred and fifty a year was wealth untold and who had never so much as heard of the Class War! For a moment he was more than half inclined to be angry with the nervous little nincompoop whose blundering, egoistic attempt at confidence had induced him to believe that the secret of his identity had been penetrated by an ardent sympathizer. (It was an open secret in “advanced” circles, though carefully guarded in the office.) Then, more justly, he softened, recognizing that the blunder was his own, the mistake of his own making﻿—and, pitying William’s dropped jaw and open confusion, poured him out a whisky and endeavoured to set him at his ease.

That evening in the company of Faraday and his first whisky was the turning-point in the career of William Tully. Any man stronger than himself could at that juncture in his life have turned him to right or left; a push in the wrong direction would have made of him an idler and a wastrel, and fate was in a kindly mood when she placed him mentally and morally in charge of “Vindex” of *The Torch*. She might, as her reckless way is, have handed over his little soul to some flamboyant rogue or expert in small vices; instead, she laid it in the keeping of a man who was clean-living, charged with unselfish enthusiasm and never consciously dishonest. The product of a Board School Scholarship and a fiercely energetic process of self-education (prompted in part by the desire to excel those he despised) Faraday, when William made his acquaintance, was beginning to realize some of his cherished ambitions, beginning, in certain Labour and Socialist circles, to be treated as a man of mark. His pen was fluent as well as sarcastic, and if his numerous contributions to the “rebel” press had been paid for at ordinary rates he would have been a prosperous journalist.

It was somewhat of a shock to William to discover on the top of the whisky that his new acquaintance was a Socialist; but after the first and momentary shock he swallowed the fact as he had swallowed the alcohol﻿—not because he liked it, but because it was something the narrow circle of his mother’s friends would have heartily and loudly disapproved of. This reactionary and undutiful attitude of mind was not deliberate or conscious; on the contrary, he would certainly have been horrified to learn that it was the dominant factor in his existence during the first few weeks of his emancipation from maternal supervision and control﻿—urging him to drink deeply of Faraday’s brand of Socialism as it urged him to partake with unnecessary sumptuousness of the best that Lyons could provide.

===Chinese===
《火炬》的温迪克斯在心里叹了口气。他年轻，野心勃勃，浑身是狠劲的认真，无时无刻不在窥伺他的「机会」；而愿望乃思想之父，他一时间把威廉错认成了这机会的化身，从早上起便做起梦来——梦见一位志同道合、甘受引领的同志，其新承的遗产尽可用来资助一份刊物，宣讲一种比《火炬》本身更纯粹、更暴烈的叛逆。凭着威廉一星期三英镑的进项——在他那热切的心眼里已放大了许多倍——他仿佛看见自己把可恨的保险业抛在身后，倾心倾笔，借阶级战争之力，投身于国家与种族的再造。可是看哪！——又有两个刨根问底的问题便叫他看清——恩主兼同志的位置上，坐着的不过是个战战兢兢的小傻瓜：头一回挣脱襻带，正自晕头转向，一年一百五十英镑在他就是无从想象的巨富，而阶级战争，他连听都没听人说起过！有一瞬间，他简直想对这战战兢兢的小傻瓜动气——正是这家伙冒冒失失、自说自话的套近乎，害他以为自己的身份之谜已被一位热切的同道识破。（在「先进」圈子里，这本是公开的秘密，虽然在办公室里被小心翼翼地捂着。）转念一想，公正些的态度占了上风，他心软了，承认这岔子出在自己身上，错是自己铸的——于是怜惜着威廉那副下巴下垂、慌相毕露的模样，给他斟了杯威士忌，设法让他自在起来。

与法拉第和他的第一杯威士忌相伴的那个夜晚，是威廉·塔利一生事业的转折点。在他一生的那个关口，任何一个比他强硬的人都能把他拨向左，或拨向右；往错道上轻轻一推，便会把他推成一个游手好闲的二流子。命运把他身心两方面都交付给《火炬》的温迪克斯照管，那会儿她心肠正软。命运素来行事莽撞，本可能把他那小小的灵魂随手交给某个浮华的骗子，或某种小恶行的行家；她却把它托付给了一个生活清正、满腔无私的热忱、从不有意为恶的人。法拉第是公学奖学金和一场炽烈用功的自学教育（一半出于压倒他所鄙视之人的欲望）造就的产物；威廉与他结识时，他正开始实现自己怀抱已久的几桩抱负，开始在工党与社会主义者的某些圈子里，被人当作一位显赫人物看待。他那支笔既泼辣又流畅；倘若他为「叛逆」报刊写下的众多稿件能按常规稿酬结算，他早就是一个殷实阔绰的记者了。

就着威士忌的后劲，发觉新相识竟是个社会主义者，这对威廉不无几分震动；但头一阵的震动过后，他把这个事实咽了下去，正如他把那酒咽了下去一样——不是因为喜欢，而是因为这桩事，他母亲那个狭小圈子里的朋友们若知道了，准会高声痛快、由衷地大加反对。这种逆反的、不孝的心态并非蓄意，也非自觉；恰恰相反，倘若有人告诉他：在摆脱母亲监督管束的头几个星期里，这心态竟是他生活的主导因素——既催着他放量痛饮法拉第牌的社会主义，也催着他穷奢极欲、大可不必地享用利昂斯餐馆所能供应的最好的菜色——他准会吓一大跳。

===Original===
He acquired the taste for Faraday’s political views more thoroughly and easily than the taste for Faraday’s whisky. The man’s authoritative and easy manner, the manner which stood him in good stead with his audiences, of assuming (quite honestly) that his statements were proven facts which no sane human being could dispute, would have made it impossible for William to combat his opinions even had his limited reading and thinking supplied him with material for the contest. He was impressed with Faraday’s erudition no less than with Faraday’s manner; and impressed still more when, later in the evening, a colleague of *The Torch* dropped in for a smoke and a chat. The pair talked Labour and International movements with the careless ease of connoisseurs and bandied the names of politicians contemptuously from mouth to mouth﻿—William sitting by in a silence dazed and awed, drinking in a language that attracted by its wild incomprehensibility and suggestion. His mind was blank and virgin for the sowing of any seed; and under Faraday’s influence his dull, half-torpid resentment against the restrictions, physical and mental, of his hitherto narrow life became merged in a wider sympathy with the general discomfort, in an honest and fiery little passion for Justice, Right and Progress. That, of course, was not the affair of one evening’s talk; but even the one evening’s talk sowed the seeds. He went away from it uncomfortably conscious that as yet he had lived solely for himself, never troubling his head concerning the evils that men like Faraday were fighting to overcome. The manner of his future living was decided for him when he knocked at the door of Faraday’s Bloomsbury lodging.

His simple and awed admiration for his newfound friend and faith had in all probability more than a little to do with Faraday’s readiness to allow the acquaintance to continue﻿—even a rebel prophet is not insensible to flattery. William became for a time his satellite and pupil, the admiring sharer of his schemes, of his hatreds and laudable ambitions; he read Faraday’s vehement articles and accepted each word and line. His very blankness of mind made him an apt pupil, and within a month of his mother’s death he was living, out of office hours, in a whirl of semipolitical agitation, attending meetings and cramming his head with pamphlets. In three months more all his hours were out-of-office hours; in his enthusiasm for his new creed and interests his neglect of his professional duties had become so marked that the manager, after one or two warnings, called him into his room for a solemn and last reprimand. As it happened, Faraday, the night before, had confided to William the news of his approaching appointment to the post of organizing secretary of the Independent Socialist Party, an appointment which would entail a speedy retirement from his hated desk in the City. The news, naturally, had not increased the attraction of the office for William, in whom the spirit of revolt had already fermented to some purpose; thus, to the infinite surprise of his superiors, who had known him hitherto as the meekest of meek little clerks, the threat of dismissal failing improvement was countered by a prompt resignation as truculent as William could make it. In fact, in the exhilaration of the moment, he treated the astonished representative of capitalism to something in the nature of a speech﻿—culled principally from the writings of “Vindex.”

From that day onwards he devoted himself to what he termed public life﻿—a ferment of protestation and grievance; sometimes genuine, sometimes manufactured or, at least, artificially heightened. He was an extremist, passionately well-intentioned and with all the extremist’s contempt for those who balance, see difficulties and strive to give the other side its due. He began by haunting meetings as a listener and a steward; Faraday’s meetings at first, then, as his circle of acquaintance and interest widened, any sort of demonstration that promised a sufficiency of excitement in the form of invective. The gentlest of creatures by nature and in private life, he grew to delight in denunciation, and under its ceaseless influence the world divided itself into two well-marked camps; the good and enlightened who agreed with him, and the fools and miscreants who did not.﻿ ﻿… In short, he became a politician.

===Chinese===
比起对法拉第威士忌的口味，他对法拉第政见的口味养成得更快，也更彻底。此人那种威严而从容的做派——那种（且是完全出于真诚地）认定自己的论断乃是不证自明、任何心智健全者都无从辩驳的事实，并因此在他听众面前屡试不爽的做派——使得威廉根本不可能同他的见解较量，纵使威廉那点有限的阅读和思考真能为他提供较量的弹药。法拉第的博学同法拉第的做派一样令他折服；更令他折服的是，晚间稍迟，一位《火炬》的同仁顺路进来抽烟闲谈。两位行家以品鉴者漫不经心的从容，大谈劳工运动与国际运动，把一干政客的名字轻蔑地在唇齿间抛来掷去——威廉坐在一旁，目瞪口呆、肃然无语地旁听，畅饮着一种语言：它那狂野的费解与暗示，正是它迷人之处。他的头脑一片空白，处女地一般，随便什么种子都能下地；在法拉第的影响下，他对自己至今为止那狭窄生活里身心种种束缚的沉闷、半僵的不满，融汇成了一种更宽广的同情——对普天下种种不自在的同情——又凝成了一场老实而火热的小小激情：为着正义、公道与进步。当然，这不是一个晚上的谈话就能了结的事；但即便这一个晚上的谈话，也已播下了种子。离开时他心中惴惴，头一回意识到：自己至今只为自己活过，对法拉第这般人物正奋力扫除的种种弊害，从不曾劳神想过。他将来以何种方式过活，在他叩响法拉第布卢姆斯伯里寓所房门的那一刻，就已替他定下了。

他对这位新交的朋友兼信仰所怀的那种单纯而敬畏的倾慕，多半与法拉第肯让这段交情继续下去大有关系——即便是造反的先知，对奉承也不是全无感觉的。有一段时期，威廉成了他的卫星和弟子，怀着倾慕分享他的宏图、他的憎恨和他可嘉的抱负；他读法拉第那些激烈的文章，一字一句照单全收。恰是他头脑的空白使他成了个一学就会的弟子；母亲去世不出一个月，他在办公时间之外，已经活在半政治性鼓动的旋涡里，四处赴会，脑袋里塞满了小册子。再过三个月，他的全部时间都成了办公时间之外的时间；他对新信仰新志趣的热情使他荒废职守的迹象日益昭彰，经理在一两次警告之后，把他叫进房间，要进行一次郑重其事的最后训诫。不巧也可巧，就在头天夜里，法拉第向威廉吐露了一个消息：他即将受任独立社会党（Independent Socialist Party）的组织书记一职，这一任命意味着他不久便可从伦敦城里那张可恨的办公桌前脱身。这消息自然不曾增加办公室对威廉的吸引力——他心里的造反精神早已酿出了些名堂；于是，当「再无起色即行辞退」的威胁摆在面前时，他以一份措辞尽其所能强硬的辞呈回敬了回去，令上司们错愕不已——在他们眼里，他向来是温顺小职员里最温顺的一个。事实上，乘着一时的亢奋，他还请那位目瞪口呆的资本主义代表欣赏了一番近似演说的东西——主要内容摘自「温迪克斯」的文章。

从那天起，他投身于他所谓的社会公共生活——一场抗议与控诉的发酵；有时出自真诚，有时是制造出来的，至少也是人工催熟的。他是个极端派，一片赤诚的好心，也怀有极端派的全套脾性：蔑视那些权衡再三的人、看见困难的人、竭力给对方一份公道的人。起初他只是泡在集会里当听众、当干事；先是法拉第的集会，后来随着交游和兴味的扩大，凡是许诺以谩骂形式提供足够刺激的示威活动，他一概到场。论天性和私下的为人，他是天下最温和的生灵，却渐渐学会了以谴责为乐；在这乐趣无休无止的熏陶下，世界在他眼前分成了界限分明的两大阵营：赞同他的善人与明白人，和不赞同他的蠢货与恶棍。……一句话，他成了政客。

===Original===
As I have said, at the outset of his career he modestly confined his energies to stewarding﻿—to the sale of what propagandist bodies insist on describing as “literature,” the taking of tickets, ushering to seats and the like; but in a short time ambition fired him and the fighting spirit thereby engendered led him to opposition meetings where, on Faraday’s advice, he tried heckling the enemy speakers. His first and flustered attempts were not over-successful, but, sustained by revolutionary enthusiasm, he refused to be crushed by failure and accompanying jeers and held doggedly on to his purpose. Repeated efforts brought their measure of success, and he began to be marked by his comrades as a willing and valuable member.

Within a year, he had found his feet and was a busy and full-blown speaker﻿—of the species that can be relied on to turn on, at any moment, a glib, excited stream of partisan fact and sentiment. His services were in constant demand, since he spoke for anything and everything﻿—provided only the promoters of the meeting were sufficiently violent in their efforts to upset the prevailing order. He had developed and was pleased with himself; Faraday, though still a great man in his eyes, was more of an equal than an idol. He was wonderfully happy in his new, unrestful existence; it was not only that he knew he was doing great good and that applause uplifted him and went to his head like wine; as a member of an organization and swayed by its collective passion, he attained to, and was conscious of, an emotional (and, as he thought, intellectual) activity of which as an individual he would have been entirely incapable. As a deceased statesman was intoxicated with the exuberance of his own verbosity, so William was intoxicated with the exuberance of his own emotions. There were moments when he looked back on his old life and could hardly believe he was the same William Tully who once, without thought of the Social Revolution, went daily from Camberwell to the City and back from the City to Camberwell.﻿ ﻿… As time went on, he was entrusted with “campaigns” and the stirring up of revolt; and it was a proud day for him when a Conservative evening paper, in connection with his share in a mining agitation, referred to him as a dangerous man. He wondered, with pity for her blindness, what his mother would have thought if anyone had told her in her lifetime that her son would turn out dangerous.

As a matter of course he was a supporter of votes for women; an adherent (equally as a matter of course) of the movement in its noisiest and most intolerant form. He signed petitions denouncing forcible feeding and attended meetings advocating civil war, where the civil warriors complained with bitterness that the other side had hit them back; and his contempt for the less virulent form of suffragist was as great as his contempt for the Home Secretary and the orthodox members of the Labour Party. It was at one of these meetings, in December 1913, that he met Griselda Watkins.

===Chinese===
正如前文所说，在事业的起步阶段，他还谦逊地把力气花在会务上——售卖那些宣传团体坚持美其名曰「文献」的东西、收票、领人入座等等；但没多久，野心便烧着了他，由此生出的斗志把他引向了对立派的集会——在那里，他遵照法拉第的指点，试着向敌方演说者起哄发难。起初那几次慌手慌脚的尝试算不上怎么成功，但有革命热情撑腰，他拒绝被失败和随之而来的讪笑压垮，倔强地朝目标顶住不放。反复的操练带来了相应的成功，同志们开始把他看作一个得力而可贵的成员。

不出一年，他站稳了脚跟，成了个大忙人、一个羽毛丰满的演说家——属于那种随时可以拧开龙头、滔滔不绝喷出一股油滑亢奋的党派事实与党派情感的种类。他的演讲常年有人延请，因为他什么题目都肯讲、什么场合都肯出席——只要集会的主办者在颠覆现行秩序的事业上足够激烈。他长进了，也颇为自得；法拉第在他眼里虽仍是位大人物，却已从偶像降作了平辈。他在这种新的、不得安生的生活里快活极了；这不仅仅因为他自知正在行大善，也不仅仅因为掌声叫他飘飘然、像酒一样直冲脑门；更因为，作为组织的一员、被组织的集体激情裹挟着，他达到——并且自知达到了——一种情感的（在他自以为还是智识的）活跃境界，这一境界，他孤身一人的时候是万万够不着的。正如某位已故的政治家醉倒在自己言辞的滔滔不绝里，威廉也醉倒在自己情感的滔滔不绝里。有些时刻，他回望旧日的生活，简直不敢相信那就是同一个威廉·塔利——此人曾经心里装不着社会革命，每日从坎伯韦尔去伦敦城，再从伦敦城回坎伯韦尔。……随着时间推移，「运动战役」和煽动造反的差事也托付到了他手上；有一家保守党的晚报，因为他在一场矿业风潮里的那一份功劳，称他为危险人物——那一天，是他引以为荣的日子。他揣度着，倘若有人在他母亲生前告诉她，她的儿子日后竟会成了危险人物，她会作何感想——并为她的有眼无珠深表惋惜。

理所当然地，他拥护妇女选举权；也（同样理所当然地）是这场运动最喧闹、最不宽容那一派的拥护者。他在谴责强制灌食的请愿书上签名，出席鼓吹内战的集会——会上，内战勇士们悲愤交加地控诉对方居然还手；他对毒性较轻的参政派女士的蔑视，不亚于他对内政大臣和工党正统成员的蔑视。正是在 1913 年 12 月的一次这类集会上，他结识了格丽泽尔达·沃特金斯（Griselda Watkins）。

===Original===
Griselda Watkins, then a little under twenty-five, was his exact counterpart in petticoats; a piece of blank-minded, suburban young-womanhood caught into the militant suffrage movement and enjoying herself therein. She was inclined to plumpness, had a fresh complexion, a mouth slightly ajar and suggestive of adenoids, and the satisfied expression which comes from a spirit at rest. Like William, she had found peace of mind and perennial interest in the hearty denunciation of those who did not agree with her.

On the night when William first saw her she wore, as a steward, a white dress, a sash with the colours of her association and a badge denoting that she had suffered for the Cause in Holloway. Her manner was eminently self-conscious and assured, but at the same time almost ostentatiously gracious and womanly; it was the policy of her particular branch of the suffrage movement to repress manifestations of the masculine type in its members and encourage fluffiness of garb and appeal of manner. Griselda, who had a natural weakness for cheap finery, was a warm adherent of the policy, went out window-smashing in a picture-hat and cultivated ladylike charm.

She introduced herself to William after the meeting with a compliment on his speech, which had been fiery enough even for her; they both considered the compliment graceful and for a few minutes exchanged sympathetic platitudes on martyrdom, civil war and the scoundrelly behaviour of the Government. Even in those first few minutes they were conscious of attraction for each other and pleased to discover, in the course of their talk, that they should meet again next week on another militant platform.

===Chinese===
格丽泽尔达·沃特金斯当时二十五岁不到一点，简直是穿了裙子的他的翻版：一块心灵空白的郊区少女料子，被激进参政运动裹挟了进去，还自得其乐。她身材略嫌丰腴，面色红润，嘴巴微微张着，透着一股腺样体肥大的意味，脸上挂着心神安定时才有的那种满足表情。同威廉一样，她也在痛痛快快地谴责异见者当中，觅得了心境的安宁和常年的兴味。

威廉初见她的那个晚上，她担任会场干事，一身白裙，斜披着本会颜色的绶带，佩着一枚徽章，表明她曾为事业在霍洛韦（Holloway）受过难。她的举止极度地自觉又笃定，但同时又近乎炫耀地和蔼可亲、女人味十足；她所属的参政运动那个特别支部的方针，便是压制成员身上男性化的做派，鼓励衣装的蓬松柔美与仪态的动人。格丽泽尔达天生偏爱廉价的花哨衣饰，对这条方针热烈拥护；她戴着阔边花帽出去砸橱窗，勤修淑女风致。

会后台主动向威廉作了自我介绍，称赞了他的演说——那场演说即便以她的标准也够火辣；两人都觉得这句恭维说得漂亮，随后几分钟里，就殉道、内战和政府的无耻行径交换了几句情投意合的陈词滥调。即便在最初那几分钟里，两人也已觉出彼此相吸，并且在交谈中发现，下星期他们还将在另一个激进派的讲坛上重逢——这发现令他们皆大欢喜。

===Original===
They met and re-met﻿—at first only on platforms, afterwards more privately and pleasantly. William, when his own meetings did not claim him, took to following Griselda about to hers, that he might listen entranced to the words of enthusiastic abuse that flowed from her confident lips; he had heard them all before and from speakers as confident, but never before had they seemed so inspired and inspiring, never before had he desired with trembling to kiss the lips that uttered them. Griselda, touched as a woman and flattered as an orator by his persistent presence in her audience, invited him to tea at her aunt’s house in Balham; the visit was a success, and from that evening (in early March) the end was a foregone conclusion. Their friendship ripened so fast that one night at the beginning of April (1914) William, escorting her home from a meeting, proposed to her on the top of an otherwise empty bus, and was duly and sweetly accepted.

There were none of the customary obstacles in the way of the happy pair﻿—on the contrary, all was plain sailing. William’s original income had for some years been augmented by his earnings as a speaker, and Griselda’s parents had left her modestly provided for. Her aunt, long since converted to the Movement (to the extent of being unable to talk of anything but forcible feeding), smiled blessings on so suitable a match and proceeded to consider the trousseau; and after a little persuasion on part the wedding was fixed for July.

===Chinese===
他们见了面，又一见再见——起初只在讲坛上，后来便更私密、更愉快了。每逢自己的集会腾不出他，威廉便开始追着格丽泽尔达赶她的一场场集会，好听得入迷地领受从她那笃定的双唇间奔涌而出的热烈咒骂；这些话他从前全听过，而且出自同样笃定的演说家之口，可它们从未显得这般如有神助、这般鼓舞人心，他也从未这般颤巍巍地渴望去吻那吐出这些话的嘴唇。格丽泽尔达身为女人被他打动，身为演说家被他捧得受用——他屡屡现身她的听众席——便请他去巴勒姆（Balham）她姨妈家喝茶；这一拜访大获成功，从那天傍晚（三月初）起，结局已是板上钉钉。两人的友谊成熟得飞快，四月头上（1914 年）的一个夜晚，威廉送她开完会回家，在一辆别无他人的公共汽车顶层上向她求婚，顺理成章、甜甜蜜蜜地被接受了。

这对幸福的人儿面前，惯常的障碍一桩也没有——恰恰相反，一路顺风顺水。威廉原本的进项，几年来又添上了他演讲挣来的钱；格丽泽尔达的父母也给她留下了一份不算丰厚、却也足够的衣食之资。她姨妈早早皈依了运动（皈依到除了强制灌食再谈不了别的的地步），笑吟吟地为这桩般配的姻缘祝福，随即着手筹划嫁妆；又稍稍劝说了几句，婚期便定在了七月。

## II / II

===Original===
The mating of William and Griselda might be called an ideal mating; theirs were indeed two hearts that beat as one. With each day they were happier in each other’s company; their minds as it were flowed together and intermingled joyously﻿—minds so alike and akin that it would have been difficult, without hearing the voice that spoke it, to distinguish an utterance of Griselda from an idea formulated by William. Their prominent blue eyes﻿—they both had prominent blue eyes﻿—looked out upon the world from exactly the same point of view; and as they had been trained by the same influences and were incapable of forming an independent judgment, it would not have been easy to find cause of disagreement between them. There are men and women not a few who find their complement in their contrast; but of such were not William and Griselda. Their standard of conduct was rigid and their views were pronounced; those who did not share their views and act in conformity with their standards were outside the pale of their liking. And this not because they were abnormally or essentially uncharitable, but because they had lived for so long less as individuals than as members of organizations﻿—a form of existence which will end by sucking charity out of the sweetest heart alive.

It was well for them, therefore, that their creed, like their code of manners and morals, was identical or practically identical. It was a simple creed and they held to it loyally and faithfully. They believed in a large, vague and beautifully undefined identity, called by William the People, and by Griselda, Woman; who in the time to come was to accomplish much beautiful and undefined Good; and in whose service they were prepared meanwhile to suffer any amount of obloquy and talk any amount of nonsense. They believed that Society could be straightened and set right by the well-meaning efforts of well-meaning souls like themselves﻿—aided by the Ballot, the Voice of the People, and Woman. They believed, in defiance of the teachings of history, that Democracy is another word for peace and goodwill towards men. They believed (quite rightly) in the purity of their own intentions; and concluded (quite wrongly) that the intentions of all persons who did not agree with them must therefore be evil and impure.﻿ ﻿… They were, in short, very honest and devout sectarians﻿—cocksure, contemptuous, intolerant, self-sacrificing after the manner of their kind.

They held, as I have said, to their own opinions strongly and would have died rather than renounce, or seem to renounce, them﻿—which did not restrain them from resenting the same attitude of mind and heart in others. What in themselves they admired as loyalty, they denounced in others as interested and malignant stubbornness. More﻿—it did not prevent them from disliking and despising many excellent persons whose opinions, if analysed, would have proved nearly akin to their own. William, for instance, would all but foam at the mouth when compulsory service in the army was the subject of conversation, and “militarism,” to him, was the blackest of all the works of the devil; but he was bitter, and violently bitter, against the blackleg who objected to compulsory service in a Trade Union, and had spoken, times without number, in hearty encouragement of that form of siege warfare which is commonly known as a Strike. He was a pacifist of the type which seeks peace and ensues it by insisting firmly, and even to blood, that it is the other side’s duty to give way.

===Chinese===
威廉与格丽泽尔达的结合，堪称理想的结合；他们确确实实是两颗跳动如一的心。他们相守一日，便快活一日；两人的头脑仿佛欢欣地汇作一处、交融一片——这头脑如此相像、如此亲近，若不听那说出口的声音，简直分不清哪句是格丽泽尔达的高论、哪条是威廉酝酿成形的见解。他们那双鼓出的蓝眼睛——两人都生着一双鼓出的蓝眼睛——从一模一样的视角打量世界；又因为塑造他们的种种影响如出一辙，两人都无力形成独立的判断，要在他俩之间找出龃龉的缘由，倒真不容易。世间不乏那样的男女，在彼此的相反相成中觅得自己的另一半；威廉与格丽泽尔达却非此道中人。他们的行为标准严格，观点旗帜鲜明；凡不赞同其观点、不遵行其标准者，都在他们好感的疆界之外。这倒不是因为他们天生或格外刻薄，而是因为他们久已不以个人、而以组织成员的身份活着——这样一种活法，到头来连天下最仁厚的心肠也要被它吸干慈爱。

因此，他们的信条，连同他们的礼法与道德守则，彼此全同或几乎全同，实在是一桩幸事。这信条简单明了，两人忠贞不贰地信守着它。他们信仰一个宏大、模糊、美得说不清道不明的存在——威廉称之为「人民」，格丽泽尔达称之为「妇女」；这个存在，来日将成就许多美好而不明确的善；而在它功德圆满之前，两人都甘愿为它受尽千般辱骂，说尽万句胡话。他们相信，社会可以靠他们这般好心肠的人的好心努力加以匡正、修葺——再辅以选票，即人民的呼声，以及妇女。他们悍然不顾历史的教诲，相信民主乃是对人类和平与善意的另一种说法。他们相信自己用心纯洁（这倒不假）；并由此断定，凡与他们意见不合者，其用心必然邪恶不洁（这就大错特错了）。……一句话，他们是极诚实、极虔诚的宗派信徒——自信满满，目中无人，党同伐异，又秉持本门本色，动辄自我牺牲。

正如前文所说，他们对自己的见解抓得极紧，宁可一死，也不肯放弃——或显得放弃了——自己的见解；但这并不妨碍他们怨恨旁人怀有同样的心态。同一种心肠，长在自己身上，他们赞之为忠诚；长在别人身上，便骂作别有用心的恶性固执。更有甚者，许多正派优秀的人物，其见解倘若细细剖析，几乎与他们自己的如出一辙，他们也照旧不喜欢、瞧不起。譬如，一谈到义务兵役，威廉几乎要口吐白沫；「军国主义」在他眼里，是魔鬼一切勾当中最黑的一桩；可是对那个拒绝服从工会强制号令的工贼，他却切齿痛恨、恨之入骨；至于那种通常名为「罢工」的围困战法，他更是演说过无数回，热忱鼓励有加。他属于那一类和平主义者：追求和平，进而以坚定的态度——甚至不惜见血——一口咬定让步是对方的责任，以此来谋求和平。

===Original===
Griselda also was a pacifist﻿—when it suited her and when she had got her way. She believed in a future World-Amity, brought about chiefly by Woman; meanwhile, she exulted loudly and frequently in the fighting qualities of her sex. Like William, she had no quarrel with Continental nations; on the contrary, what she had seen of Continental nations during a fortnight’s stay at Interlaken had inclined her to look on them with favour. Like William, her combatant instincts were concentrated on antagonists nearer home; she knew them better and therefore disliked them more. It is a mistake to suppose that either nations or individuals will necessarily like the people they see most of; if you must know a man in order to love him, you seldom hate a man with whom you have no acquaintance. Nothing could have been more ideally peaceful than the relations of China and England during the Middle Ages﻿—for the simple reason that China and England knew absolutely nothing of each other. In the same way, if in a lesser degree, Griselda and William had a friendly feeling for Germany and the German people. They had never been to Germany and knew nothing of her history or politics; but they had heard of the Germans as intelligent people addicted to spectacles, beer and sonatas, and established on the banks of the Rhine. And﻿—the Rhine being some way off﻿—they liked them.

As internationalists they had no words too strong for standing armies and their methods; but upon military operations against domestic tyrants they looked with less disapproval. There existed, I believe, in the back of their minds some ill-defined distinction between bloodshed perpetrated by persons clad in uniform and by persons not so clad﻿—between fighting with bayonets and fighting with bombs and brickbats. The one was militarism and unjustifiable; the other heroism and holy. Had you been unkind enough to pen them into a corner and force them to acknowledge that there are many born warriors out of khaki, they would have ended probably by declaring that one should take arms only against tyranny and in a righteous cause﻿—and so have found themselves in entire agreement not only with their adversary but with the Tory Party, the German Emperor, the professional soldier and poor humanity in general. The elect, when one comes to examine them, are not always so very elect. The difficulty would have been to persuade them that there could be two opinions concerning a cause they espoused; their little vision was as narrow as it was pure, and their little minds were so seldom exhausted by thinking. Apostles of the reign of Woman and of International Amity, they might have been summed up as the perfect type of aggressor.

With regard to what used to be called culture (before August 1914), the attainments of William and Griselda were very much on a level. They read newspapers written by persons who wholly agreed with their views; they read pamphlets issued, and books recommended, by societies of which they were members. From these they quoted, in public and imposingly, with absolute faith in their statements. Of history and science, of literature and art, they knew nothing, or next to nothing; and, their ignorance being mutual, neither bored the other by straying away from the subjects in which both were interested.﻿ ﻿… As I have said, their mating was an ideal mating.

===Chinese===
格丽泽尔达也是个和平主义者——在合她心意的时候，在她如愿以偿的时候。她信仰一个主要由妇女一手促成的未来世界亲善；与此同时，她又时时高声欢呼自己性别的好斗品格。同威廉一样，她对欧陆各国并无嫌隙；恰恰相反，在因特拉肯（Interlaken）小住两周期间所见的欧陆各国，反倒使她对她们心生好感。同威廉一样，她的好斗本能集中于离家更近的对头身上；她对这些人更知根知底，因而也就更不喜欢他们。以为国家或个人必定喜欢他们最常照面的人，这是一种错误；倘若必须先认识一个人才能爱他，那么你也极少会恨一个素不相识的人。中世纪期间，中国与英格兰的关系再理想和平不过了——道理很简单：两者彼此绝对一无所知。同理——只是程度稍浅——格丽泽尔达与威廉对德国和德国人民也怀着一番友好情谊。他们从未去过德国，对德国的历史与政治一无所知；但他们听说德国人聪明伶俐，嗜好眼镜、啤酒和奏鸣曲，定居在莱茵河（Rhine）畔。而且——莱茵河毕竟远在天边——他们喜欢德国人。

作为国际主义者，他们对常备军及其手段，用什么狠话骂都不嫌重；可是对讨伐国内暴君的军事行动，他们的非难就轻得多了。我相信，在他们脑海深处，存在着某种说不清道不明的区分：穿制服的人流的血是一回事，不穿制服的人流的血是另一回事——用刺刀打仗是一回事，用炸弹和砖块打仗是另一回事。前者是军国主义，罪不可赦；后者是英雄主义，神圣无比。你若狠得下心，把他们逼进死角，逼他们承认不穿卡其军装的天生战士也大有人在，他们多半到头来会声明：唯有反抗暴政、投身正义之业，方可诉诸武力——如此一来，他们便发现自己不但同论敌完全一致，而且同托利党、德皇、职业军人乃至普天下可怜的芸芸众生完全一致了。上帝的选民，细究起来，未必总那么出众。难就难在让他们相信：他们拥护的事业居然还容得下第二种意见；他们那小小的眼界纯洁而狭窄，小小的头脑又难得被思考累着。身为妇女当政与国际亲善的传道者，他们却大可以被概括为侵略者的完美典型。

至于从前（1914 年 8 月以前）所谓的文化，威廉与格丽泽尔达的造诣可谓不相上下。他们读的报纸，出自与他们见解完全一致的笔者之手；他们读的小册子和书，都是他们所入社团印发和推荐的。他们引经据典，当众卖弄，气派十足，对其中的论断奉若神明。至于历史与科学、文学与艺术，他们一无所知，或近乎一无所知；好在这无知是彼此彼此，谁也不会撇下两人共同的兴趣、旁骛别处，叫对方听得打哈欠。……正如前文所说，他们的结合是理想的结合。

===Original===
The period of their engagement was not without its beauty; an ever-present consciousness of their mission to mankind did not prevent them from being blissful as loving young couples are blissful﻿—it merely coloured their relations and spiritualized them. One evening, not long before their wedding, they sat together in Battersea Park on a bench and dedicated their mutual lives to the service of Progress and Humanity. They had invented a suitable formula for the occasion and repeated it softly, one after the other, holding each other’s hands. Griselda’s voice trembled as she vowed, in semi-ecclesiastical phraseology, that not even her great love for William should wean her from her life’s work; and William’s voice shook back as he vowed in his turn that not even Griselda, the woman of his dreams, should make him neglectful of the call of Mankind and his duty to the holiest of causes. It was a very solemn little moment; man and woman, affianced lovers, they dedicated themselves to their mission, the uplifting of the human race. They were spared the doubts which would have assailed wiser heads as to the manner of accomplishing their mission; and as they sat side by side on the bench, with their hands clasped, they knew themselves for acceptable types and forerunners of the world they were helping to create.﻿ ﻿… Man and Woman, side by side, vowed to service.

“We shall never forget this evening,” Griselda whispered as the sun dipped down in glory. “In all our lives there can be nothing more beautiful than this.”

She was right; the two best gifts of life are love and an approving conscience. These twain, William and Griselda, loved each other sincerely﻿—if not with the tempestuous passion of a Romeo and a Juliet, with an honest and healthy affection; they had for each other an attraction which could set their pulses beating and start them dreaming dreams. That evening, on the bench in Battersea Park, they had dreamed their dreams﻿—while their consciences looked on and smiled. They foreshadowed their home not only as a nest where they two and their children should dwell, but as a centre of light and duty﻿—as they understood duty and light; a meeting-place for the like-minded, where fresh courage could be gathered for the strife with prejudice and evil. They pictured themselves (this was in June 1914) as what they would have called Powers﻿—as a man and a woman working for progress and destined to leave their mark. The sense of their destiny awed and elated them﻿—and they walked away from Battersea Park with their hearts too full for speech.

===Chinese===
订婚的时日自有其美好之处；那种对人类使命的念念不忘，并不妨碍他们像一切热恋的年轻情侣那样幸福——只是给他们的关系添了色彩，使之崇高化了。婚礼前不久的一个傍晚，两人同坐在巴特西公园（Battersea Park）的一张长椅上，将彼此的生命奉献给进步与人类的事业。他们为这个场合编好了一套得体的套语，执手相握，一先一后轻声诵念。格丽泽尔达用半教堂式的措辞起誓：纵使她对威廉爱得再深，也绝不能使她离开毕生的事业——她的声音颤抖着；轮到威廉起誓：纵使格丽泽尔达是他梦中的女子，也绝不能使他漠视人类的召唤和对最神圣事业的职责——他的声音也颤抖着回应。这是一个郑重其事的小小时刻；一男一女，一对订了婚的恋人，把自身献给了他们的使命——提升全人类。至于如何完成这一使命，更聪明的头脑难免要遭受怀疑的侵袭，他们却幸免于此；他们并肩坐在长椅上，双手相扣，自知是合格的时代典型，是他们正参与缔造的那个新世界的先驱。……男人和女人，并肩而坐，盟誓效忠。

「我们永远忘不了今天晚上。」夕阳壮丽地沉落时，格丽泽尔达低声说。「我们一生中，再没有比这更美的了。」

她说得对；人生最好的两件礼物，一是爱，一是无亏的良心。这两个人，威廉与格丽泽尔达，彼此真心相爱——纵然没有罗密欧与朱丽叶那种疾风骤雨的激情，却有一种诚实而健全的深情；他们相互吸引，一碰就脉搏加速，做起梦来。那天傍晚，在巴特西公园的长椅上，他们做完了各自的梦——他们的良心在一旁观望着，微笑着。他们预想中的家，不只是他们两口子和孩子栖身的窠巢，还是一座光明与本分的中心——按他们对本分和光明的理解；一个同道中人的聚会之所，在那里，人们可以养精蓄锐，再赴与偏见和邪恶的搏斗。他们把自己（这是 1914 年 6 月）想象成他们所谓的「力量」——一对为进步效力、注定要留下印迹的男女。命运感令他们又敬畏又昂扬——他们走出巴特西公园时，心头发烫，说不出话来。

===Original===
On the way home a flaring headline distracted Griselda temporarily from her dreams. “Who’s this Archduke that’s been assassinated?” she asked. (Her morning’s reading had been confined to *The Suffragette*.)

“Austrian,” William informed her. (He had read the *Daily Herald*.) “Franz Joseph﻿—no, Franz Ferdinand﻿—the heir to the Austrian throne.”

“Who assassinated him?” his betrothed inquired, not very much interested.

“I can’t remember their names,” William admitted, “but there seem to have been several in it. Anyhow, he’s been assassinated. Somewhere in the Balkans. With bombs.”

===Chinese===
回家的路上，一条刺眼的大标题把格丽泽尔达从梦中暂时拽了出来。「这个被刺杀的大公是谁？」她问。（她早晨的读物只限于《妇女参政者》（The Suffragette）。）

「奥地利人。」威廉告诉她。（他读的是《每日先驱报》（Daily Herald）。）「弗朗茨·约瑟夫——不对，是弗朗茨·斐迪南——奥地利皇位的继承人。」

「谁刺杀的他？」他的未婚妻问道，兴味缺缺。

「名字我记不得了。」威廉承认，「但凶手好像有好几个。反正他是被刺杀了。在巴尔干什么地方。用的是炸弹。」

===Original===
“Oh!” said Griselda, ceasing to be interested at all. Her mind had turned from traffic in strange archdukes and was running on a high resolve; the solemn vow of service was translating itself into action.

“I shall go to the meeting tomorrow,” she announced, “and make my protest.”

William knew what was passing in her mind and made no effort to dissuade her. No more than she dared he let their mutual happiness enervate them﻿—it must urge them to high endeavour, to struggle and sacrifice for the Cause.

“I’ll go too,” he said simply, “if I can manage to get a ticket.”

===Chinese===
「哦！」格丽泽尔达说，这下兴味全无。她的心思已从陌生大公的种种传闻上转开，正奔着一个高昂的决心而去；那效忠的庄严誓言，正在化为行动。

「明天那个会我要去，」她宣布，「去提我的抗议。」

威廉明白她心里翻涌着什么，也不去劝阻。同她一样，他也不敢让他们共同的幸福消磨了两人的锐气——幸福必须激励他们奋进，为事业去斗争、去牺牲。

「我也去，」他直截了当地说，「要是能弄到票的话。」

===Original===
“Oh, I’ll get you a ticket,” Griselda told him; “they’re sure to have some at the office”﻿—and thanked him with a squeeze of the fingers that set his pulses beating.

She was as good as her word, and the next night saw him in a Cabinet Minister’s audience. From his seat in the arena (their seats were not together and the pair had entered separately) his eye sought for Griselda and found her easily in the first row of the balcony﻿—most obviously composed and with her gloved hands folded on the rail. She was dressed in pale blue, with a flowered toque perched on her head; her blue silk blouse, in view of possibilities, was firmly connected by safety-pins with the belt of her blue cloth skirt, and her hair secured more tightly than usual by an extra allowance of combs. Previous experience had taught her the wisdom of these measures. As usual, in accordance with the tradition of her party, she had insisted in her costume on the ultra-feminine note; her blouse savoured of Liberty and there was a cluster of rosebuds at her breast. She was breathing quickly, so her mouth was more open than usual; otherwise she gave no sign of mental or physical trepidation﻿—save a studied indifference which might have betrayed her to an eye sufficiently acute. To William she looked adorable and his heart swelled with admiration of her courage and determination to sustain her in her protest to the uttermost; he vowed to himself to be worthy of such a mate.

===Chinese===
「哦，票我来弄，」格丽泽尔达对他说；「办公室肯定有富余的」——说着捏了捏他的手指致谢，这一捏叫他脉搏加速。

她说到做到，第二天晚上，他便坐进了一位内阁大臣的听众席。从他场子里的座位（两人的座位不在一起，进场也各走各的）举目搜寻，他一眼便在楼厅第一排找到了格丽泽尔达——神色泰然自若，戴手套的双手交叠在栏杆上。她一身淡蓝，头上歪戴一顶小花帽；那件蓝绸衬衫，鉴于种种可能的变故，已用别针牢牢同蓝呢裙的腰带连为一体，头发也比平日多插了几把梳子，扎得格外紧牢。以往的经验教会了她这番措施里的大智慧。同往常一样，遵照她那一党的传统，她的装束坚持了极尽女性化的调子；那件衬衫颇有几分利伯蒂（Liberty）绸的气韵，胸前簇着一团玫瑰花蕾。她呼吸急促，嘴便比平时张得更开些；除此以外，她不露一丝精神或身体的慌张——只有一副刻意的淡漠，落在足够尖的眼睛里，反倒会泄露天机。在威廉眼里，她可爱极了；他心中涨满了对她勇气的钦佩，以及支撑她把抗议进行到底的决心；他暗暗发誓，要配得上这样的伴侣。

===Original===
He did his best to prove himself worthy when the critical moment came. He waited for that moment during more than three-quarters of an hour﻿—for Griselda was not without confederates, and three ladies in picture hats and a gentleman in the garb of a Nonconformist minister had arisen at intervals to make the running before her voice rang out. All were suppressed, though not without excitement; two of the ladies parted with their hats and the clergyman broke a chair. The chair and the clergyman having been alike removed, the audience buzzed down into silence, and for full five minutes there was peace﻿—until the speaker permitted himself a jesting allusion to the recently exported objectors. A man with a steward’s rosette in his coat was stationed in the gangway close to William; and as the laughter the jest had provoked died away, he swore under his breath, “By God, there’s another in the balcony!” William swung round, saw Griselda on her feet and heard her voice shrill out﻿—to him an inspiration and a clarion, to the steward a source of profanity.

“Mr. Chairman, I rise to protest against the speaker’s gross insult to the noble women who﻿—”

A man in the seat behind clapped his hands on her shoulders and rammed her back into her chair﻿—where she writhed vigorously, calling him coward and demanding how he dared! His grip, sufficiently hard to be unpleasant, roused her fighting instincts and gave a fillip to her conscientious protest; in contact with actual, if not painful, personal violence, she found it easier to scream, hit out and struggle. Two stewards, starting from either end of the row of chairs, were wedging themselves towards her; she clung to her seat with fingers and toes, and shrieked a regulation formula which the meeting drowned in opprobrium. Conscious of rectitude, the jeers and hoots but encouraged her and fired her blood; and when her hands were wrenched from their hold on the chair she clung and clawed to the shoulder of her next-door neighbour﻿—a stout and orthodox Liberal who thrust her from him, snorting indignation. One steward had her gripped under the armpits, the other with difficulty mastered her active ankles; and, wriggling like a blue silk eel and crowing her indefatigable protest, she was bundled in rapid and businesslike fashion to a side entrance of the building.

===Chinese===
关键时刻来临时，他竭力证明自己当得起。他等这一刻等了三刻钟还多——因为格丽泽尔达并非没有同党：三位戴阔边花帽的女士和一位穿非国教牧师装束的先生，已轮番起身打头阵，而后才轮到她的声音响起。个个都被镇压了下去，虽说场面上颇热闹了一阵；两位女士丢了帽子，那位牧师折断了一把椅子。椅子和牧师被一并请出去之后，听众嗡嗡着静了下来，足足太平了五分钟——直到演说者禁不住打趣了一句，暗讽那批新近「出境」的抗议者。过道里、离威廉不远，站着一个衣领上佩着干事绶带的男人；那句玩笑引来的笑声刚落，他便压着嗓子咒骂：「天杀的，楼厅里又冒出一个！」威廉猛地转身，看见格丽泽尔达已站起身，听见她的声音尖利地划破会场——在他听来，那是神启，是号角；在干事听来，那是脏话的催化剂。

「主席先生，我起立抗议演说者对高贵女性的粗鄙侮辱，她们——」

后排一个男人双手按住她的肩膀，把她硬生生掼回椅子里——她在椅中奋力扭动，骂他是懦夫，质问他怎么敢！那一按足够狠，按得人不好受，却激起了她的斗志，给她凭良心的抗议又添了一把火；一旦亲历了这真实算不上疼痛的肢体暴力，她喊叫、挥拳、挣扎起来反倒更顺当了。两名干事从座椅排的两端向她挤过来；她手指脚趾一齐抠住座位，尖声喊出一句规定套语，旋即被满场的羞辱声淹没。她自觉理直气壮，讥笑和倒彩反而给她打气，叫她热血沸腾；等到双手被从椅背上掰开，她便一把抓住邻座男人的肩膀，指爪并用——那是个敦实的、正统的自由党人，愤愤地喷着响鼻，把她推了开去。一名干事架住她的腋下，另一名好不容易制服她那双不肯安分的脚踝；她像一条蓝绸鳗鱼似的扭动，不休不倦地高唱抗议，被麻利干练地一股脑儿押到了大楼的侧门。

===Original===
“Cowards!” she ejaculated as she found her feet on the pavement.

“Damned little cat!” was the ungentlemanly rejoinder. “If you come here again I’ll pare your nasty little nails for you.”

And, dabbing a scored left hand with his handkerchief, the steward returned to his duties﻿—leaving Griselda in the centre of a jocular crowd attracted to the spot by several previous ejections. She was minus her rosebuds, her toque and quite half of her hairpins; on the other hand, she held tightly grasped in her fingers a crumpled silk necktie which had once been the property of a stout and orthodox Liberal. She was conscious that she had acted with perfect dignity as well as with unusual courage﻿—and that consciousness, combined with her experience of similar situations, enabled her to sustain with calm contempt the attentions of the jocular crowd.

===Chinese===
「懦夫！」她踏上人行道，迸出这么一句。

「该死的小疯猫！」回答的可没什么绅士风度。「你再敢来，我就替你把那几只贼指甲修一修。」

那干事用手帕按了按被抓破的左手，回岗位去了——留下格丽泽尔达站在一圈哄笑的人群中央；先前的几次逐人出场，早把这群看热闹的引到了这里。她的玫瑰花蕾、小花帽和足足半数发夹都不见了；不过另一方面，她手里还紧紧攥着一条皱成一团的绸领带——那原是某位敦实的、正统的自由党人的财产。她自觉方才不仅勇气超群，举止更是体面到了十分——这份自觉，加上她对类似场面的丰富经验，使她能在哄笑人群的围观跟前，报以平静的蔑视。

===Original===
“You’d like a taxi, I suppose, miss?” the constable on duty suggested﻿—having also considerable experience of similar situations. Griselda assented and the taxi was duly hailed. Before it arrived at the kerb she was joined on the pavement by her lover, who had left the meeting by the same door as his betrothed and in much the same manner and condition; he had parted with a shoe as well as a hat, and one of his braces was broken. A hearty shove assisted him down the steps to the pavement where, to the applause of the unthinking multitude, he fell on his knees in an attitude of adoration before Griselda’s friend the constable. Recovering his equilibrium, he would have turned again to the assault; but his game attempt to reenter the building was frustrated not only by a solidly extended arm of the law but by the intervention of Griselda herself.

“You have done enough for tonight, dear,” she whispered, taking his arm. “My instructions are not to insist on arrest. We have made our protest﻿—we can afford to withdraw.”

She led the retreat to the taxi with a dignity born of practice; William, now conscious of his snapped brace, following with less deportment. The vehicle once clear of the jeering crowd, Griselda put her arms round her lover and kissed his forehead solemnly.

===Chinese===
「小姐，您怕是要叫辆出租汽车吧？」值勤的警察提议道——他对类似场面也颇有几分阅历。格丽泽尔达表示同意，出租汽车随即被招呼了过来。车还没到路边，她的情人已来到她身边——他也同未婚妻一样，从同一扇门、以大致相同的方式和状态离开了会场：不但丢了帽子，还搭上一只鞋，背带也断了一条。结结实实的一推，把他送下台阶，送上人行道；在不动脑筋的看客们的喝彩声中，他双膝跪倒，摆出一副五体投地的姿态，拜在格丽泽尔达的朋友——那位警察——面前。他稳住身子，还想再杀回去；可他那股硬骨头的拼劲，不但被法律的一条铁臂挡了回去，也被格丽泽尔达本人的干预拦了下来。

「今晚你出的力够多了，亲爱的。」她挽起他的手臂，低声说。「我得到的指示是：不必坚持被捕。抗议我们已经提过了——我们撤得起。」

她以久经操练养成的威仪，率领撤退上了出租汽车；威廉这时才觉出断了的那条背带，跟在后面，仪态就差了些。车子一驶出讪笑的人群，格丽泽尔达便搂住她的情人，庄重地吻了吻他的额头。

===Original===
“My dear one,” she said, “I am proud of you.”

“Oh, Griselda, I’m proud of you,” he murmured between their kisses. “How brave you are﻿—how wonderful!﻿—how dared they!﻿ ﻿… I went nearly mad when I saw them handling you﻿—I hit out, and the cowards knocked me down.﻿ ﻿… A woman raising her voice on the side of justice﻿—and they silence her with brutal violence﻿—”

“It’s only what we must expect, dear,” she whispered back, stroking his rumpled hair. “Remember this is War﻿—God knows it’s horrible, but we must not shrink from it.”

She spoke from her heart, from the profound ignorance of the unread and unimaginative﻿ ﻿… and once more in the darkness of the taxi the warriors clasped and kissed.

===Chinese===
「我亲爱的，」她说，「我为你骄傲。」

「哦，格丽泽尔达，我才为你骄傲呢。」他在亲吻的间隙里喃喃道。「你多勇敢——多了不起！——他们怎么敢！……我看见他们对你动手，差点疯了——我挥拳打过去，那帮懦夫把我打倒。……一个女人，在正义一边发出声音——他们竟用野蛮的暴力叫她噤声——」

「这不过是我们必须料到的，亲爱的。」她低声回答，抚着他蓬乱的头发。「记住，这是战争——天知道它可怕，可我们不能退缩。」

她说的是心里话——出自不读书、无想象力者的深渊般的无知……于是，在出租汽车的黑暗里，这对勇士又一次相拥相吻。

## III / III

===Original===
After the usual hesitations and excursions they had settled on their future home﻿—a tiny flat in Bloomsbury, central and handy for the perpetual getting about to meetings which was so integral a part of their well-filled, bustling lives. They furnished it lovingly and with what they considered good taste; Griselda brought in her friends to admire, and engaged a respectable woman who was to “do” for them and have all in readiness when they returned from their four weeks’ honeymoon﻿—and they were as foolishly happy over their nest as any other loving little couple.

They were married towards the end of July﻿—to be exact, on the twenty-third day of the month. The wedding took place in Balham from the house of Griselda’s aunt; the ceremony was performed by an enlightened vicar who had consented to omit the ignoble vow of obedience; and the church was thronged to its doors with comrades and ardent sympathizers. The advanced Press spread itself over the description of the ceremony and﻿—in view of the fact that the bridesmaids, six in number, had all done time for assault﻿—even the Press that was not advanced considered the event worth a paragraph. The pair were snapshotted on leaving the church with the customary direful results, and the modest residence of Griselda’s aunt could hardly accommodate the flood of progressive guests. There was rice and slipper-throwing and a whirl of good wishes﻿—and Griselda, flushed, looked pretty, and in William’s eyes quite lovely.

They left by an afternoon train for Dover and crossed the next day to Ostend.

---

===Chinese===
经过惯常的犹豫与迂回，他们终于选定了未来的家——布卢姆斯伯里一套小小的公寓，地段居中，往各处会场奔波起来再便利不过；这种没完没了的赶会，正是他们那充实热闹的生活须臾不可分的一部分。他们怀着爱、也怀着他们自以为的好品味，把它布置起来；格丽泽尔达领来朋友们参观称赞，又雇了一个体面的妇人，替他们「打理」家务，好等他们四星期蜜月归来时一切停当——对着这个窠巢，他们像天下任何一对恩爱的小夫妻一样，傻乎乎地幸福着。

他们于七月底成婚——确切说，是那个月的二十三日。婚礼在巴勒姆格丽泽尔达姨妈的宅子里操办；主礼的是一位开明的牧区牧师，答应略去「服从」那条不体面的誓词；教堂里挤满了同志和热烈的同道，直挤到门口。先进的报刊连篇累牍地铺写了婚礼的盛况——鉴于六位伴娘个个都因伤人罪坐过牢——连不先进的报界也觉得此事值得一小段。两人走出教堂时被抢拍了快照，效果一如往常地惨不忍睹；格丽泽尔达姨妈那座朴素的宅子，几乎装不下进步宾客的洪流。撒米的，扔鞋的，好话连成旋风——格丽泽尔达红着脸，显得怪好看的，在威廉眼里简直是美极了。

他们乘午后的火车离开，前往多佛尔（Dover），次日渡海去奥斯坦德（Ostend）。

---

===Original===
Their selection of the Belgian Ardennes for a honeymoon was due to Griselda’s long-standing acquaintance with a cosmopolitan female revolutionist understood to be of Russian Polish extraction. Owing, it was further understood, to her pronounced opinions and pronounced manner of expressing them, she had long ceased to be welcome in the land that gave her birth; at any rate, she avoided it studiously and existed chiefly at a series of epoch-making revolutionary meetings which she addressed by turns in bad German, worse French, and worst English. She wrote vehement pamphlets in all these languages and prided herself on the fact that, on the Continent at least, they were frequently suppressed by the police; wore tartan blouses, a perennial smile, and a hat that was always askew. For some reason or another she was the possessor of a cottage in the heart of the Belgian Ardennes﻿—which she visited on the rare occasions when she was not plying her epoch-making activities in London, Vienna, or New York. A week once a year was about all the use she made of it﻿—reappearing after her seven days’ seclusion with a brace of new pamphlets burning for the Press, and like a giant refreshed for the fight. This estimable woman was as good-natured as she was revolutionary, and hearing that Griselda was thinking of a rural honeymoon, she hastened to offer the happy couple the loan of her Belgian property, which was as secluded as heart could desire. Griselda, since her fortnight at Interlaken, had hankered for another stay abroad; she jumped at the offer, and William﻿—whose acquaintance with foreign parts was limited to an International Socialist Congress in Holland﻿—jumped gladly in unison with her. Madame Amberg beamed with joy at their delighted acceptance of her offer; she kissed Griselda, shook hands with William and promised to make all the necessary arrangements for their stay﻿—to write to the old woman who looked after the cottage and tell her when to expect them. She babbled rhapsodically of honeymoons and the joys of the Forest of Arden﻿—forgetting how bored she was in the Forest of Arden at the end of a seven-days’ stay there﻿—and further directed them how to reach it, looked out trains and suggested hotels.

Heaven smiled on the opening of their married life; Dover was a receding beauty in the distance and the Channel a good-natured lake. As their boat chunked between the long piers of Ostend they held and squeezed each other’s hands ecstatically, the crowd collecting on the gangway side enabling them to do so unnoticed. The consciousness of their total ignorance of the language of the country gave them an agitated moment as they set foot on foreign soil﻿—but, taken in tow by a polyglot porter, they were safely transferred from the quay to a second-class carriage, with instructions to change at Brussels; and, having changed obediently, were in due course set down at Namur. Madame Amberg had advised them to lodge with economy at the Hotel de Hollande near the station; but another specimen of the polyglot porter pounced down and annexed them firmly for his own more distant establishment, and after a feeble resistance they followed him meekly and were thrust with their bags into the omnibus lying in wait for them. They felt it their duty as the vehicle rattled them along to make a few depreciatory and high-principled remarks on the subject of the towering fortifications; but having thus satisfied their consciences they relapsed into mere enjoyment of rest, novelty, a good dinner, and a view of the lazy Meuse. After dinner, when the fortress above them was fading into the soft blackness of a warm summer night, they walked arm-in-arm by the river and were quite unutterably happy.

They rose early in the morning to catch the little river boat for Dinant; caught it with the aid of their guardian, the porter, and camped side by side on its deck to enjoy the sauntering trip. They enjoyed it so much and engrossingly that for the space of a morning they forgot their high principles, they forgot even Woman and Democracy; they were tourists only, agape and delighted, with their green Cook’s tickets in their pockets. Yet, after all, they were something more than tourists; they were young man and woman who loved each other tenderly and whose happy lives were in tune with the happy landscape. Often they forgot to look at the happy landscape for the joy of gazing into each other’s dear blue eyes.

===Chinese===
他们选中比利时（Belgium）的阿登（Ardennes）做蜜月地，是因为格丽泽尔达结识多年的一位世界主义女革命家——据说出身俄属波兰。据说还由于她观点激烈、表达观点的方式更激烈，她在生养她的故土早已不受欢迎；总之，她小心避开故土，主要活在一系列划时代的革命集会里，轮流用蹩脚的德语、更蹩脚的法语和最蹩脚的英语登台演讲。她用这几种文字写下激烈的小册子，并引以为荣——至少在欧陆，它们经常被警察查禁；她穿格子呢衬衫，挂着常年不谢的微笑，帽子永远歪着。不知什么缘故，她在比利时阿登的腹地拥有一座小屋——只有当她不在伦敦、维也纳（Vienna）或纽约（New York）从事划时代活动的稀有时节，才去光顾一下。一年大约也只住上一星期——七天与世隔绝的日子过后重新露面，带回两本急待付印的新小册子，如巨人般养精蓄锐，再赴战斗。这位可敬的妇人性情和善，一如其革命；听说格丽泽尔达有意去乡下度蜜月，她连忙提出把比利时的产业借给这对幸福的人儿——那地方僻静得很，正合心坎里的想望。自从因特拉肯两周之行后，格丽泽尔达一直渴望再去国外住住；她一口应承下来；威廉——他的国外见识仅限于在荷兰开过一回国际社会党人大会——也欣然同她一齐跳了起来。见他们欢天喜地接受了提议，安伯格夫人（Madame Amberg）笑逐颜开；她吻了格丽泽尔达，同威廉握了手，答应替他们打点逗留期间的一切安排——写信给照看小屋的老妇人，告诉她两位何时光临。她如痴如醉地絮叨着蜜月和阿登森林（Forest of Arden）的种种妙处——全忘了她自己在那儿待满七天之后是如何厌烦——又细细指点他们如何抵达，查好了火车班次，推荐了旅馆。

上苍对他们婚后生活的开端颇为垂青；多佛尔成了一抹远去的倩影，英吉利海峡是一面好脾气的湖。轮船喀嚓喀嚓驶进奥斯坦德两道长堤之间时，两人如痴如醉地攥紧了彼此的手；舷梯一侧聚起了人，正好让他们这般亲热而不被察觉。踏上外国土地的那一刻，想起自己对这个国家的语言一窍不通，两人心慌了一阵——但一位通晓数国语言的搬运工把他们接管过去，平平安安地从码头送进一节二等车厢，嘱咐他们到布鲁塞尔（Brussels）换车；他们乖乖换过车，不久便在那慕尔（Namur）被放了下来。安伯格夫人原劝他们省着点，住车站附近的荷兰旅馆（Hotel de Hollande）；可另一位通晓数国语言的搬运工猛扑过来，把他们牢牢截下，拉向他自家更远的旅店；他们微弱地抵抗了一下，便温顺地跟他走了，连人带包被塞进候在那儿的公共马车。马车辘辘载着他们前行，两人觉得有义务就那高耸的炮台工事发表几句高义的贬斥；良心既已交代，他们便重新沉入纯粹的享受——休憩之乐、新奇之乐、一顿好饭，还有那条懒洋洋的默兹河（Meuse）。晚饭后，头顶的要塞正融进温暖夏夜的柔软黑影里，他们挽着臂沿河散步，幸福得难以言表。

一早他们起身，去赶开往迪南（Dinant）的小火轮；在守护神搬运工的帮助下赶上了船，并肩在甲板上安营扎寨，享受这段慢悠悠的航程。他们享用得如此投入，以致整整一个上午忘了自己的高尚原则，甚至忘了妇女和民主；他们只是游客，看得张口结舌、心花怒放，口袋里揣着绿色的库克（Cook）通票。然而说到底，他们又不止是游客；他们是一对彼此深爱的青年男女，幸福的生命正与幸福的山水同调。他们常常忘了看那幸福的山水，只顾凝望彼此可爱的蓝眼睛。

===Original===
The boat puffed finally to Dinant, where they stayed the night as planned; where they stared at the cupola’d church and the cliffs, walked to the split rock on the road to Anseremme, and bought some of the gingerbread their guidebook had told them to buy. They ate it next day, with no particular approval, on the final stage of their journey, in a train that puffed and pottered between heights and orchards in the winding company of a stream. It puffed and pottered them at last to their wayside destination﻿—where a smiling and loutish country boy slouched up to take possession of them and their modest baggage. They understood not a word of his thick-throated patois, but knew from information imparted beforehand by Madame Amberg that this was their housekeeper’s grandson and deputed to serve as their guide. He gripped a bag easily in either hand, and led the way past the few small houses ranged neatly as a miniature village alongside the miniature station﻿—and so by the white road that kept the river company. After a mile or thereabout they left the white road, turning sharply to the right at a cleft in the riverside cliff and striking a cart-track, scarcely more than a path, into a valley twisted back among the hills.

It was a valley the like of which they had never seen, which the world seemed to have forgotten; a cool green vision of summer and solitary peace. Water had cleft in the tableland above them a passage that time had made leafy and gracious and laid aside for their finding. Through the flat, lush pastures that divided the bold slopes there looped and tangled a tiny brook on its way to the Meuse and the sea, a winding ribbon of shadow, of shimmer and reflection. Up the slopes rising steeply from the pastures there clustered tree above tree﻿—so thickly set that where the valley dwindled in the distance they might have been moss on the hillside. There was no one in sight and no sound but their footsteps and the birds; it was all a green prettiness given over to birds and themselves.

“Isn’t it wonderful?” Griselda said, not knowing that her voice had dropped and lost its shrillness. “I never thought there could be such a place.”

===Chinese===
船最后喷着气到了迪南，他们按计划在那儿过夜；在那儿，他们仰望圆顶的教堂和峭壁，走到通往昂瑟雷姆（Anseremme）路上那块裂开的岩石，又买了些导游手册嘱咐他们买的姜饼。第二天，在旅程的最后一段，他们嚼着那姜饼，吃得不怎么领情；火车在群山和果园之间吭哧吭哧、慢慢腾腾，与一条小溪结伴蜿蜒。它终于把他们吭哧到了路边的目的地——一个笑嘻嘻、傻乎乎的乡下小子晃晃悠悠地迎上来，接管了他们和他们简朴的行李。他那喉咙浓重的一口土话，他们一个字也听不懂，但凭安伯格夫人预先提供的情报，知道这是管家婆的孙子，奉命给他们当向导。他两手各拎一只提包，轻轻松松领路先行，走过紧挨着小车站排成一列、宛如微缩村庄的几座小屋——再沿一条与河相伴的白路前行。走了约一英里，他们离开白路，在河边峭壁的一道裂口处向右急转，踏上一条勉强算得上路的手推车道，钻进群山深处迂回扭转的一道山谷。

那是一道他们从未见过的山谷，世界仿佛早已把它忘记；一幅清凉碧绿的幻境，满是夏天与孤独的安宁。流水在头顶的高原上劈出一道裂谷，时间把它装点得枝叶葱茏、温婉可亲，又搁置在一旁，专候他们前来寻访。分割陡坡的那些平展肥沃的牧场间，一条小溪回环缠绕，一路奔向默兹河和大海，一条蜿蜒的缎带，光影闪烁，倒影迷离。从牧场陡然升起的坡上，树挨着树、簇拥成团——在那山谷远去渐窄的地方，层层叠叠的树简直像山坡上的青苔。四望无人，除了他们的脚步声便是鸟鸣；一谷清翠的秀美，只交给飞鸟和他们两人受用。

「这地方多好啊。」格丽泽尔达说，没觉出自己的嗓音已放低、失了尖利。「我从没想到世上会有这样的地方。」

===Original===
She spoke the truth, if in hackneyed and unthinking phrase; in her busy and crowded little mind, the reflection of her busy little life, there had been no room for visions of a deep and solitary peace. Involuntarily, as they walked they drew nearer together and went closely side by side; the sweet aloofness of the valley not only amazed them, it awed them; they were dimly conscious of being in contact with something which in its silent, gracious way was disquieting as well as beautiful. Their theory and practice of life, so far, was the theory and practice of their purely urban environment, of crowds, committees and grievances and cocksure little people like themselves﻿—and lo, out of an atmosphere of cocksureness and hustle they had stepped, as it seemed without warning, into one of mystery and the endless patience of the earth. Out here, in this strange overpowering peace, it was difficult to be conscious of grievances political or ethical; instead came a new, undefined and uneasy sense of personal inadequacy and shrinkage, a sense of the unknown and hitherto unallowed-for, a fear of something undreamed of in their rabid and secondhand philosophy.﻿ ﻿… Not that they reasoned after this fashion, or were capable of analysing the source of the tremor that mingled with their physical pleasure, their sheer delight of the eye; but before they had been in the valley many hours they sympathized in secret (they did not know why) with Madame Amberg’s consistent avoidance of its loveliness, saw hazily and without comprehension why, for all her praise of its beauties, she was so loth to dwell there. The place, though they knew it not, was a New Idea to them﻿—and therefore a shadow of terror to their patterned and settled convictions. As such their organized and regulated minds shrank from it at once and instinctively﻿—cautiously apprehensive lest the New Idea should tamper with accepted beliefs, disturb established views and call generally for the exercise of faculties hitherto unused. They had an uneasy foreboding﻿—never mentioned aloud by either, though troublous to both﻿—that long contact with solitude and beauty might end by confusing issues that once were plain, and so unfitting them for the work of Progress and Humanity﻿—for committees, agitations, the absorbing of pamphlets and the general duty of rearranging the universe.

There was something, probably, in the frame of mind in which they approached the valley; they came of a migratory, holiday race, and had seen green beauty before, if only fleetingly and at intervals. What they had lacked before was the insight into beauty born of their own hearts’ content, the wonder created by their own most happy love.﻿ ﻿… They followed their guide for the most part in silence, relieved that he had ceased his well-meaning attempts to make them understand his jargon, and speaking, when they spoke at all, in voices lowered almost to a whisper.

“What’s that?” Griselda queried, still under her breath. “That” was a flash of blue fire ahead of them darting slantwise over the stream. Later they learned to understand that a flash of blue fire meant kingfisher; but for the moment William shook his head, nonplussed, and hazarded only, “It’s a bird.”

===Chinese===
她说的句句是实话，虽说用语陈腐、未经思索；在她那忙碌拥挤的小脑袋里——那是她忙碌小生活的映照——从来不曾有过深沉幽静之安宁的位置。走着走着，两人不由自主地越靠越近，紧紧并肩而行；山谷甜美的幽僻不仅令他们惊叹，还令他们敬畏；他们隐约觉出，自己正触到一样东西，它以其静默温婉的方式，既美得动人，又令人不安。他们迄今为止的人生理论与实践，纯然是他们那彻底城市环境——人群、委员会、控诉，以及他们这种自信满满的小人物——的理论与实践；可是看哪，仿佛全无预警，他们竟从自负与喧嚣的空气里，一步踏进了另一种空气——神秘与大地之无限耐心的空气。在这陌生而摄人的安宁里，要想保持对政治或伦理怨气的自觉，竟成了难事；取而代之的，是一种新奇的、说不清的不自在——自觉个人的不足与萎缩，自觉冥冥中有未知之物、有他们那狂热而二手的哲学从不曾预算过的东西，对它生出几分惧意。……这倒不是说他俩能这般推理，能剖析那份渗进肉体快感、渗进纯然悦目之乐里的震颤的来源；但在谷中待了还没几个小时，他们便暗暗地（自己也说不清为什么）体谅起安伯格夫人一贯回避这片可爱山水的行径，迷迷瞪瞪、不明所以地看出：她尽管把这里的美景夸了又夸，却为何总不肯在此久住。这地方，虽说他们自己并不知道，对他们是一个全新的观念——因而也是他们那刻板定型之信仰的一片恐怖的阴影。正因如此，他们那被组织、被规训过的头脑当即本能地畏缩了——小心翼翼地提防着，唯恐这新观念染指既定的信条，搅动固有的成见，并进而要求动用他们从未用过的官能。两人心里都有一层不安的预感——谁也没说出口，可谁都觉得堵得慌——生怕与孤独和美的长久厮磨，到头来会把原本清清楚楚的是非搅成一团混沌，使他们再不配担当进步与人类的事业——再不配委员会、鼓动、吞小册子，以及重新安排宇宙的总职责。

他们在走近山谷时的心境，想必也有些讲究；他们本是一个惯于迁徙、惯于度假的种族的儿女，从前也见过绿色的美景，只是匆匆一瞥、时断时续罢了。他们从前缺少的，是发自内心满足的对美的洞见，是他们自己最幸福的爱情所创造的奇迹。……他们跟着向导走，多半一言不发；他那些好心的、想叫他们听懂他的土话的努力既已作罢，两人倒觉轻松；偶尔开口，嗓音也压得几乎成了耳语。

「那是什么？」格丽泽尔达仍在低声问。「那」是一星蓝色的火光，在他们前方斜掠过溪流。后来他们才学会：一星蓝火便是翠鸟；可当时威廉只能困惑地摇摇头，试探着说了句：「是只鸟。」

===Original===
For half-an-hour or so it was only the birds and themselves; then at a turn in the narrowing valley they came in sight of cows nuzzling the pastures. Several cows, parti-coloured black and white like the cows of a Noah’s Ark; and, further on, a tiny farm house standing close up to the trees in its patch of vegetable garden. They knew from Madame Amberg of the existence of the tiny farmhouse; it was an old woman living there who would “do” for them during their stay at the neighbouring cottage﻿—cook and clean and make tidy in return for a moderate wage. The barking of a kennelled nondescript brought the old woman shuffling to the door﻿—to welcome them (presumably) in her native tongue and to take their measure from head to heel with a pair of shrewd, sunken eyes. Of her verbal greetings they understood nothing but the mention of Madame Amberg; but, having looked them up and down enough, the old lady shuffled back into her kitchen for the key of the revolutionist’s property. She reappeared with the key in one hand and a copper stewpan in the other﻿—wherewith she waved the signal to advance, and shuffled off in guidance, ahead of her grandson and the visitors. A quarter of an hour’s more walking on a dwindling path brought them in sight of their sylvan honeymoon abode; it had originally been built for the use of a woodman, and was a four-roomed cottage on the edge of the wood overlooking the stream and the pasture of the black-and-white cows. Madame Peys (they knew at least her name) unlocked the door and ushered them into the kitchen, where the boy deposited their bags.

===Chinese===
如此走了约莫半个钟头，一路只有鸟儿和他们俩；随后，在渐窄山谷的一个转弯处，他们望见了在牧地上拱嘴的奶牛。好几头奶牛，黑白花斑，像诺亚方舟里的玩具牛；再往前，一座小小的农舍紧挨着树丛，立在它那一畦菜园里。从安伯格夫人处他们已知有这座小农舍；住在里面的是位老妇人，他们逗留邻近小屋期间，将由她来替他们「打理」——做饭、打扫、收拾，换取一份适中的工钱。一条拴着的不知什么狗叫了几声，把老妇人召唤到门口——她拖着步子出来，用她的母语（想必是）欢迎他们，一双精明凹陷的眼睛把他们从头到脚打量了一遍。她口头的话他们一句不懂，只听出「安伯格夫人」几个字；老太太把他们上上下下瞧够了，便拖着步子回厨房去取那位革命家产业的钥匙。她再露面时，一手钥匙，一手一只铜炖锅——用锅一挥，示意前进，随即拖拖着步子领头，走在孙子和客人前头。沿一条越走越窄的小径再走一刻钟，他们便望见了林中蜜月的居所；它起初本是给一个伐木工住的，是林子边上的一座四间小屋，俯临溪流和黑白花牛的牧场。佩斯夫人（Madame Peys）——他们至少知道她的名字——开了锁，引他们进厨房，小子把行李撂在了那儿。

## IV / IV

===Original===
The cottage was what anyone who knew Madame Amberg might have expected her cottage to be. It was sparsely furnished, except with explosive literature; there were very few chairs, and those few verging on decrepitude, but numerous tracts and pamphlets in divers civilized languages. Kitchen utensils were conspicuous chiefly by their absence, and presumably the owner relied consistently on the loan of the copper stewpan which had accompanied her guests from the farm. On the other hand, the kitchen walls were adorned by photographs, more or less flyblown, of various political extremists, and a signed presentment of Rosa Luxembourg adorned a bedroom mantelpiece.

While Madame Peys made play with the stewpan and a kettle, the honeymoon couple unpacked their bags and examined their new domain: still, to a certain extent, overawed by the silence and loneliness around it; still, unknown to themselves, speaking more gently and with more hesitation than usual. It was the familiar tang of the books and pamphlets, with which the shelves were crammed and the floor was heaped, that first revived their quieted spirits and created a sense of home. Woman and Democracy, even on the backs of books, had power to act as a tonic and trumpet-call, to reflect the atmosphere of noise and controversy where alone they could breathe with comfort. With unconscious relief they turned from the window and the prospect of the valley, green and untenanted, to entrench themselves against the assaults of the unknown behind the friendly and familiar volumes that had overflowed from Madame Amberg’s deal bookshelves to Madame Amberg’s uncarpeted floor. Conning them, handling them and turning their pages, they were again on the solid ground of impatient intolerance; they were back amongst their own, their cherished certainties. William in the presence of Belfort Bax felt his feet once more beneath him; Griselda, recognizing a pamphlet by Christabel Pankhurst, ceased to be troubled by the loneliness around her, grew animated and raised her voice.﻿ ﻿… And the savoury mess which Madame produced from her stewpan, combined with the no less excellent coffee that followed, dispelled for the moment the sense of mystery and the shadow of the New Idea.

===Chinese===
这座小屋，正是任何认识安伯格夫人的人料想中她的小屋该有的样子。屋里家具寥寥，唯独「爆炸性文献」琳琅满目；椅子极少，仅有的几把也行将就木，各文明语言的教册与小册子却为数众多。厨房家什更是以缺席著称；想来主人一贯仰仗借用那只铜炖锅——也就是随她这两位客人从农舍来的那一只。不过，厨房墙上装饰着各色政治极端分子的照片——或多或少的蝇屎斑驳，卧室壁炉台上还供着一帧罗莎·卢森堡（Rosa Luxemburg）的签名照。
